#13 — Dynamic Programming Patterns

Chapter 09 taught the machinery of DP: define a subproblem, write a recurrence, pick base cases, choose an iteration order. This chapter teaches recognition — the handful of recurring shapes that most interview DP problems fall into, so you can spot which one you're looking at and reach for the matching template.

Builds on: the memoisation/tabulation foundations from Dynamic Programming (Introduction). If "what does dp[i] mean?" isn't yet second nature, go back first — here we assume it is and focus on the patterns.

#Core Concepts

#The four DP shapes you'll meet most

ShapeThe decision at each stepCanonical problem
0/1 choiceTake this item or skip it0/1 Knapsack
Sequence / subsequenceExtend or restart a runLongest Increasing Subsequence
Grid / 2DCome from above or from the leftUnique Paths, Min Path Sum
Two-stringCharacters match or they don'tLongest Common Subsequence, Edit Distance

Recognising the shape tells you the dimensions of your dp table and the form of the recurrence before you write a line of code.

#The recognition question

When you suspect DP (from chapter 09: "number of ways", "min/max cost", "is it possible"), ask "what is the decision being made repeatedly, and what does the answer depend on?" The answer defines your state:

  • Depends on an index and a remaining capacity → 0/1 knapsack shape (2D: item × capacity).
  • Depends on the best run ending here → subsequence shape (1D over positions).
  • Depends on a position in a grid → grid shape (2D over rows × cols).
  • Depends on two positions, one in each string → two-string shape (2D).

#Patterns with Code

#Pattern 1: 0/1 Knapsack — take-it-or-leave-it with a budget

Task: Given items with a weight and a value, and a bag with a weight limit, choose items (each at most once) to get the highest total value.

Example: weights = [1, 3, 4], values = [15, 20, 30], capacity = 4 → 35 (take the items with weights 1 and 3)

You have items with weights and values and a capacity. Each item is used at most once. Maximise value without exceeding capacity. The state is dp[i][w] = best value using the first i items with capacity w.

int Knapsack(int[] weights, int[] values, int capacity)
{
    int n = weights.Length;
    // dp[i, w] = max value using first i items within capacity w
    int[,] dp = new int[n + 1, capacity + 1];

    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++)
        for (int w = 0; w <= capacity; w++)
        {
            // Option A: skip item i → inherit the value without it.
            dp[i, w] = dp[i - 1, w];

            // Option B: take item i (if it fits) → its value + best of remaining capacity.
            if (weights[i - 1] <= w)
                dp[i, w] = Math.Max(dp[i, w],
                                    values[i - 1] + dp[i - 1, w - weights[i - 1]]);
        }

    return dp[n, capacity];
}
def knapsack(weights, values, capacity):
    n = len(weights)
    dp = [[0] * (capacity + 1) for _ in range(n + 1)]

    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        for w in range(capacity + 1):
            dp[i][w] = dp[i - 1][w]                     # skip item i
            if weights[i - 1] <= w:                      # or take it if it fits
                dp[i][w] = max(dp[i][w],
                               values[i - 1] + dp[i - 1][w - weights[i - 1]])
    return dp[n][capacity]

The shape: a 2D table indexed by which items × how much budget. The recurrence is always "best of (skip) vs (take, if it fits)". Subset-sum, partition-equal-subset, and target-sum are all this shape in disguise.

#Pattern 2: Subsequence — "best run so far" (LIS)

Task: Find the length of the longest strictly increasing subsequence (the numbers don't have to be next to each other).

Example: nums = [10, 9, 2, 5, 3, 7] → 3 (for example 2, 5, 7)

(Full walkthrough: Problem 2 below.)

For Longest Increasing Subsequence, dp[i] = length of the longest increasing subsequence ending at index i. Each position looks back at all smaller-value predecessors and extends the best one.

int LengthOfLIS(int[] nums)
{
    if (nums.Length == 0) return 0;
    // dp[i] = length of the longest increasing subsequence ending at i.
    int[] dp = new int[nums.Length];
    Array.Fill(dp, 1);          // each element alone is a subsequence of length 1
    int best = 1;

    for (int i = 1; i < nums.Length; i++)
        for (int j = 0; j < i; j++)
            if (nums[j] < nums[i])                 // can we extend the run ending at j?
            {
                dp[i] = Math.Max(dp[i], dp[j] + 1);
                best = Math.Max(best, dp[i]);
            }

    return best;
}
def length_of_lis(nums):
    if not nums:
        return 0
    dp = [1] * len(nums)        # each element is an LIS of length 1 by itself
    best = 1
    for i in range(1, len(nums)):
        for j in range(i):
            if nums[j] < nums[i]:                   # extend the run ending at j
                dp[i] = max(dp[i], dp[j] + 1)
                best = max(best, dp[i])
    return best

The shape: 1D table over positions, where dp[i] summarises "the best thing ending here". The answer is often the max over all dp[i], not dp[last]. (This is O(n²); an O(n log n) version using binary search exists — a nice follow-up that reuses chapter 06.)

#Pattern 3: Grid — accumulate from neighbours (Min Path Sum)

Task: In a grid of costs, find the cheapest path from the top-left to the bottom-right, moving only right or down.

Example: grid = [[1,3,1],[1,5,1],[4,2,1]] → 7 (the path 1 → 3 → 1 → 1 → 1)

On a grid, dp[i][j] is the best cost to reach cell (i,j), built from the cells you could have come from (above or left). This generalises Unique Paths from chapter 09 (counting) to optimising a cost.

int MinPathSum(int[][] grid)
{
    int rows = grid.Length, cols = grid[0].Length;
    int[,] dp = new int[rows, cols];
    dp[0, 0] = grid[0][0];

    // First row: can only come from the left.
    for (int c = 1; c < cols; c++) dp[0, c] = dp[0, c - 1] + grid[0][c];
    // First column: can only come from above.
    for (int r = 1; r < rows; r++) dp[r, 0] = dp[r - 1, 0] + grid[r][0];

    // Interior: cheaper of (from above) vs (from left), plus this cell.
    for (int r = 1; r < rows; r++)
        for (int c = 1; c < cols; c++)
            dp[r, c] = grid[r][c] + Math.Min(dp[r - 1, c], dp[r, c - 1]);

    return dp[rows - 1, cols - 1];
}
def min_path_sum(grid):
    rows, cols = len(grid), len(grid[0])
    dp = [[0] * cols for _ in range(rows)]
    dp[0][0] = grid[0][0]

    for c in range(1, cols):                    # first row: from the left only
        dp[0][c] = dp[0][c - 1] + grid[0][c]
    for r in range(1, rows):                    # first column: from above only
        dp[r][0] = dp[r - 1][0] + grid[r][0]

    for r in range(1, rows):
        for c in range(1, cols):
            dp[r][c] = grid[r][c] + min(dp[r - 1][c], dp[r][c - 1])  # best neighbour
    return dp[rows - 1][cols - 1]

The shape: 2D table matching the grid; fill the edges as base cases, then each interior cell combines its reachable neighbours. Watch for the base-case edges — they're where grid DP usually breaks.

#Pattern 4: Two strings — match or mismatch (Edit Distance)

Task: Find the minimum number of insert, delete or replace steps needed to turn one string into another.

Example: "horse" → "ros" takes 3 steps

When a problem compares two sequences, the state is dp[i][j] over a prefix of each. Edit Distance: minimum insert/delete/replace operations to turn string a into string b.

int EditDistance(string a, string b)
{
    int m = a.Length, n = b.Length;
    // dp[i, j] = edits to turn a[0..i) into b[0..j)
    int[,] dp = new int[m + 1, n + 1];

    // Base cases: turning a prefix into "" costs that many deletions/insertions.
    for (int i = 0; i <= m; i++) dp[i, 0] = i;
    for (int j = 0; j <= n; j++) dp[0, j] = j;

    for (int i = 1; i <= m; i++)
        for (int j = 1; j <= n; j++)
        {
            if (a[i - 1] == b[j - 1])
                dp[i, j] = dp[i - 1, j - 1];            // characters match → no cost
            else
                dp[i, j] = 1 + Math.Min(dp[i - 1, j - 1],      // replace
                                 Math.Min(dp[i - 1, j],        // delete from a
                                          dp[i, j - 1]));      // insert into a
        }

    return dp[m, n];
}
def edit_distance(a, b):
    m, n = len(a), len(b)
    dp = [[0] * (n + 1) for _ in range(m + 1)]

    for i in range(m + 1):      # base: delete all of a's prefix
        dp[i][0] = i
    for j in range(n + 1):      # base: insert all of b's prefix
        dp[0][j] = j

    for i in range(1, m + 1):
        for j in range(1, n + 1):
            if a[i - 1] == b[j - 1]:
                dp[i][j] = dp[i - 1][j - 1]             # match → carry over
            else:
                dp[i][j] = 1 + min(dp[i - 1][j - 1],    # replace
                                   dp[i - 1][j],        # delete
                                   dp[i][j - 1])        # insert
    return dp[m][n]

The shape: 2D table over prefixes of two strings; the recurrence always splits into "last characters match" vs "they don't" (and in the mismatch case, the three edit operations). Longest Common Subsequence is the same skeleton with a different recurrence.

#Practice Problems

#Problem 1 — Partition Equal Subset Sum (LeetCode #416)

Task: Can the array be split into two subsets with equal sum?

Example: [1, 5, 11, 5] → true ([1,5,5] and [11])

Key insight: This is 0/1 knapsack in disguise. If the total is odd, it's impossible. Otherwise the question becomes: "can we pick a subset that sums to total / 2?" — a take-it-or-leave-it decision per element.

Solution:

bool CanPartition(int[] nums)
{
    int total = nums.Sum();
    if (total % 2 != 0) return false;          // odd total can't split evenly
    int target = total / 2;

    // dp[s] = can we form sum s using some subset seen so far?
    var dp = new bool[target + 1];
    dp[0] = true;                               // empty subset sums to 0

    foreach (int num in nums)
        // Iterate downward so each item is used at most once (0/1, not unbounded).
        for (int s = target; s >= num; s--)
            dp[s] = dp[s] || dp[s - num];

    return dp[target];
}
def can_partition(nums):
    total = sum(nums)
    if total % 2 != 0:
        return False
    target = total // 2

    dp = [False] * (target + 1)
    dp[0] = True
    for num in nums:
        for s in range(target, num - 1, -1):   # downward → use each item once
            dp[s] = dp[s] or dp[s - num]
    return dp[target]

⏱ Time: O(n × target) | Space: O(target)

#Problem 2 — Longest Increasing Subsequence (LeetCode #300)

Task: Return the length of the longest strictly increasing subsequence.

Example: [10, 9, 2, 5, 3, 7, 101, 18] → 4 ([2, 3, 7, 101])

Solution: (Pattern 2 directly.)

int LengthOfLIS(int[] nums)
{
    int[] dp = new int[nums.Length];
    Array.Fill(dp, 1);
    int best = 1;
    for (int i = 1; i < nums.Length; i++)
        for (int j = 0; j < i; j++)
            if (nums[j] < nums[i])
            {
                dp[i] = Math.Max(dp[i], dp[j] + 1);
                best = Math.Max(best, dp[i]);
            }
    return nums.Length == 0 ? 0 : best;
}
def length_of_lis(nums):
    if not nums:
        return 0
    dp = [1] * len(nums)
    for i in range(1, len(nums)):
        for j in range(i):
            if nums[j] < nums[i]:
                dp[i] = max(dp[i], dp[j] + 1)
    return max(dp)

⏱ Time: O(n²) | Space: O(n)

#Problem 3 — Longest Common Subsequence (LeetCode #1143)

Task: Return the length of the longest subsequence common to two strings.

Example: "abcde", "ace" → 3 ("ace")

Key insight: Two-string shape. dp[i][j] = LCS length of the first i chars of a and first j of b. If the current characters match, extend the diagonal; otherwise take the best of dropping one character from either string.

Solution:

int LongestCommonSubsequence(string a, string b)
{
    int m = a.Length, n = b.Length;
    int[,] dp = new int[m + 1, n + 1];          // dp[0, *] and dp[*, 0] = 0 (empty)

    for (int i = 1; i <= m; i++)
        for (int j = 1; j <= n; j++)
            if (a[i - 1] == b[j - 1])
                dp[i, j] = dp[i - 1, j - 1] + 1;        // match → extend diagonal
            else
                dp[i, j] = Math.Max(dp[i - 1, j], dp[i, j - 1]);  // drop one char

    return dp[m, n];
}
def longest_common_subsequence(a, b):
    m, n = len(a), len(b)
    dp = [[0] * (n + 1) for _ in range(m + 1)]

    for i in range(1, m + 1):
        for j in range(1, n + 1):
            if a[i - 1] == b[j - 1]:
                dp[i][j] = dp[i - 1][j - 1] + 1          # match → extend
            else:
                dp[i][j] = max(dp[i - 1][j], dp[i][j - 1])  # drop a char
    return dp[m][n]

⏱ Time: O(m × n) | Space: O(m × n)

#Problem 4 — Coin Change II (LeetCode #518)

Task: Given coin denominations and an amount, return the number of combinations that make up that amount (unlimited coins each).

Example: amount = 5, coins = [1, 2, 5] → 4

Key insight: Contrast with chapter 09's Coin Change (fewest coins). This counts combinations, and the loop order matters: iterate coins on the outside so you count each combination once (not every ordering). This is the unbounded-knapsack counting variant.

Solution:

int Change(int amount, int[] coins)
{
    // dp[a] = number of combinations that sum to a.
    var dp = new int[amount + 1];
    dp[0] = 1;                      // one way to make 0: use nothing

    // Coins OUTSIDE, amount inside → combinations, not permutations.
    foreach (int coin in coins)
        for (int a = coin; a <= amount; a++)
            dp[a] += dp[a - coin];

    return dp[amount];
}
def change(amount, coins):
    dp = [0] * (amount + 1)
    dp[0] = 1                       # one way to make 0
    for coin in coins:              # coins outer loop = count combinations once
        for a in range(coin, amount + 1):
            dp[a] += dp[a - coin]
    return dp[amount]

⏱ Time: O(amount × coins) | Space: O(amount)

#From Recurrence to Code: a repeatable recipe

  1. Name the state in a sentence: "dp[i][j] = …". If you can't, you don't understand the problem yet.
  2. Match it to a shape (0/1 choice, subsequence, grid, two-string) → that fixes the table dimensions.
  3. Write the recurrence as "best/count of the choices available at this state".
  4. Fill base cases — usually the empty prefix / first row / first column.
  5. Pick the order so every value you read is already computed.
  6. Optimise space last — many 2D tables collapse to 1D (as in Partition Equal Subset Sum).

#Common Mistakes

  • Wrong loop direction for 0/1 vs unbounded — iterate capacity downward to use an item once (0/1); upward to reuse it (unbounded). Getting this backwards silently changes the problem.
  • Coins/amount loop order — for counting combinations, coins must be the outer loop; swapping counts permutations instead.
  • Answer is max over dp, not dp[last] — true for LIS and similar "best ending here" problems.
  • Off-by-one on string DP — size tables (m+1) × (n+1) and index the string with i-1/j-1.
  • Skipping base cases — the first row/column is where grid and string DP most often break.

#LeetCode Problems to Try Now

  • #416 — Partition Equal Subset Sum ⭐ (0/1 knapsack)
  • #300 — Longest Increasing Subsequence ⭐ (subsequence shape)
  • #1143 — Longest Common Subsequence ⭐⭐ (two-string shape)
  • #518 — Coin Change II ⭐⭐ (unbounded counting)
  • #64 — Minimum Path Sum (grid shape)
  • #72 — Edit Distance ⭐⭐ (two-string, three operations)
  • #1049 — Last Stone Weight II (subset-sum in disguise)

#14 — Pattern Recognition & Interview Method

This is the capstone. Chapters 01–13 each taught a tool. This chapter teaches the two skills that actually decide a coding interview: recognising which tool a new problem needs, and communicating your way through it. Owning twelve patterns is worthless if you freeze when a fresh prompt doesn't announce which one applies — and it never does.

Builds on: every prior chapter. Keep them open; this chapter constantly points back to them.

#Why this matters more than solving more problems

In a Monzo-style pairing interview, the interviewer is watching how you think, not whether you've memorised a solution. They want to see you:

  • turn a vague prompt into a precise problem,
  • recognise the underlying pattern quickly,
  • reason about trade-offs out loud,
  • write clean code and test it yourself.

A candidate who deeply owns ~13 patterns and talks through them clearly beats one who has "seen" 300 problems but goes silent. So this chapter is about recognition speed and a repeatable method, not more volume.

#The Recognition Cheat Sheet

Train yourself to read a problem and map its signals to a pattern. These signals are the single most useful thing to memorise.

If the problem says / looks like…Reach forChapter
"sum/pair/duplicate", fast lookups neededHash map / set02
Sorted array, find a pair/triplet, or remove in placeTwo pointers03 · mastery
Contiguous subarray/substring with a conditionSliding window04
Matching/nesting, "most recent", next-greaterStack (monotonic)05
Sorted input + "find/search", or "minimise the max"Binary search06
Reorder/detect-cycle in a chain, "in place" pointersLinked-list pointers07
Tree shape, "depth/path/validate", mirrorTree DFS/BFS + recursion08
"number of ways", "min/max cost", "is it possible"Dynamic programming09 · 13
Nodes & edges, grid, "connected"/"reachable"/"shortest unweighted"Graph BFS/DFS / Union-Find10
"top/K largest/smallest", "median", "merge K"Heap / priority queue11
"all combinations/permutations/subsets", "generate every"Backtracking12
"maximum non-overlapping", "fewest jumps", "sort then pick"Greedy15
"starts with", "autocomplete", "prefix", "dictionary of words"Trie (prefix tree)16

#Secondary tells

  • "Shortest path, unweighted" → BFS specifically (not DFS) — BFS reaches nodes in distance order (10).
  • "Shortest/cheapest path with weights" (no negatives) → Dijkstra = BFS with a min-heap (10).
  • "Greedy feels right" → check it's provably safe (exchange argument); otherwise it's probably DP (15 vs 13).
  • "Top K" with k ≪ n → a size-K heap, not a full sort (11).
  • "Contiguous" → sliding window; "any order / subsequence" → usually DP or backtracking. That one word changes the pattern.
  • "Sorted" appearing anywhere is a loud hint for two pointers or binary search.
  • A grid is almost always a graph in disguise (10).

#The Interview Method (say this out loud, every time)

This is the framework from the interview-prep track, made into muscle memory. Narrate each step — the interviewer scores your reasoning, not just your final code.

1. CLARIFY      Restate the problem. Ask about input size, ranges, duplicates,
                empty/null, sorted?, return value. Never start coding blind.
2. EXAMPLES     Walk a concrete example by hand. Then an edge case
                (empty, single element, all-same, negative).
3. BRUTE FORCE  State the naive approach and its complexity — out loud.
                "I could do this in O(n²) by…". Shows you can always ship *something*.
4. OPTIMISE     Name the pattern. "Since the array is sorted, two pointers
                gets this to O(n)." This is where chapters 01–13 pay off.
5. CODE         Write it cleanly, talking as you go. Good names, no dead code.
6. TEST         Trace your own code on the example. Check the edge cases from step 2.
                Fix bugs before they tell you.

Golden rule: a working brute force you can explain beats an optimal solution you get stuck halfway through. Always secure an answer, then improve it.

#Worked Walkthrough — applying the method cold

Let's run the method on a prompt as if seeing it for the first time.

Prompt: "Given an array of integers, return the length of the longest substring… I mean subarray… whose elements sum to at most k. All numbers are positive."

1. Clarify. "Positive integers only — good. Contiguous subarray, not subsequence? (yes.) Can k be negative or zero? If all numbers are positive and k ≥ 0, an empty subarray trivially sums to 0. Return the length, not the subarray itself?"

2. Examples. nums = [2, 1, 1, 3], k = 4 → [2, 1, 1] sums to 4, length 3. Edge: k = 0 → length 0 (positives can't fit). Single element bigger than k → 0.

3. Brute force. "I could check every subarray — O(n²) pairs, each summed in O(1) with running totals. That works but it's quadratic."

4. Optimise — recognise the pattern. The word "contiguous" + "a condition on the sum" is the sliding-window tell (chapter 04). Because all numbers are positive, the sum grows as the window expands and shrinks as it contracts — so a variable window is valid. "I'll expand right, and while the sum exceeds k, shrink from left. O(n)."

5. Code.

int LongestSubarrayAtMostK(int[] nums, int k)
{
    int left = 0, sum = 0, best = 0;
    for (int right = 0; right < nums.Length; right++)
    {
        sum += nums[right];                 // expand the window
        while (sum > k && left <= right)    // shrink while invalid
            sum -= nums[left++];
        best = Math.Max(best, right - left + 1);
    }
    return best;
}

6. Test. Trace [2,1,1,3], k=4: window grows to [2,1,1] (sum 4, len 3); adding 3 → sum 7 > 4, shrink until valid. best = 3. ✓ Edge k=0: the while empties every window → best = 0. ✓

Notice the pattern was never named in the prompt — "contiguous" + "sum condition" + "positive numbers" is what pointed to sliding window. That inference is the skill.

#How to practise recognition (not just solving)

Solving problems one by one builds tools; this routine builds recognition:

  1. Read 10 problems without solving them. For each, write only: "Pattern = _, because _." Check against editorials. You'll be wrong early — that's the point.
  2. Group by pattern, not by difficulty. Do five sliding-window problems in a row; the shared structure becomes obvious and the tell sticks.
  3. Re-derive, don't re-memorise. After solving, close the editorial and rewrite from the pattern, not from memory of that specific solution.
  4. Time the recognition, not the coding. Give yourself 60 seconds per prompt to name the pattern and the target complexity out loud. Interviews are won or lost in that first minute.
  5. Keep a "signals" log. Each time a problem surprises you, add its tell to your own version of the cheat sheet above.

#Reading complexity out loud

Interviewers expect you to state complexity unprompted. A quick reference tied to the patterns:

PatternTypical timeTypical space
Hash map / setO(n)O(n)
Two pointers / sliding windowO(n)O(1)
Binary searchO(log n)O(1)
Tree/graph BFS/DFSO(V + E)O(V)
Heap (top-K)O(n log k)O(k)
Backtrackingexponential (prune!)O(depth)
DPO(states × transition)O(states)

If your approach is worse than these for its pattern, that's usually the hint you've missed the intended optimisation.

#Common Mistakes (interview behaviour, not just code)

  • Coding before clarifying — you solve the wrong problem. Always run step 1.
  • Silent thinking — the interviewer can't score what they can't hear. Narrate.
  • Jumping to "the clever trick" — state the brute force first; it's your safety net.
  • Not testing your own code — trace an example before they ask. Catching your own bug is a strong signal.
  • Pattern-forcing — not everything is DP. Match signals to patterns honestly; sometimes a hash map is the whole answer.
  • Giving up at the optimisation — a clear O(n²) you can explain is a pass; a half-written O(n) you abandoned is not.

#Where to go next

  • Drill by pattern using the 50-problem plan in the Personal Projects — Coding Practice Plan.
  • For the interview conversation itself, see the interview-prep Coding & Algorithms chapters, which apply this exact method to fintech-flavoured prompts.
  • Then shift to System Design — a separate skill with its own method, covered in the interview-prep track and the System Design Building Blocks chapter.

#15 — Greedy Algorithms

A greedy algorithm builds an answer one step at a time, always taking the choice that looks best right now, and never reconsidering. When that local optimum also gives the global optimum, greedy is the simplest and fastest tool you have — often turning an exponential search into a single O(n) or O(n log n) pass.

Builds on: 06 — Sorting & Binary Search (most greedy solutions start by sorting) and 09 — Dynamic Programming Intro (the contrast that tells you when greedy is safe).

#Core idea — commit and never look back

DP explores every choice and keeps the best; greedy makes one choice at each step and moves on. That's why greedy is dramatically cheaper — and also why it's dangerous: if an early choice was wrong, greedy can't undo it.

Greedy  : pick best-looking option now → never revisit  → fast, sometimes wrong
DP      : try all options, keep the best combination     → slower, always correct

The whole skill is knowing when the greedy choice is provably safe. Two properties must hold:

  1. Greedy-choice property — a globally optimal solution can be reached by making a locally optimal choice at each step.
  2. Optimal substructure — after making that choice, what remains is a smaller instance of the same problem.

If you can't convince yourself both hold, reach for DP instead.

#When to reach for greedy vs DP

SignalLikely toolWhy
"Maximum number of non-overlapping …" / schedulingGreedySort by end time; earliest finish leaves the most room
"Minimum number of … to cover/reach"Greedy (often)Reach as far as possible each step
"Number of ways" / "count the combinations"DPGreedy can't count; it commits to one path
"Min/max cost" where a locally bad step enables a better futureDPGreedy's no-takebacks rule fails here
Coins/amounts with arbitrary denominations (min coins)DPGreedy fails (see Common Mistakes)
Coins with canonical denominations (real currency)Greedy worksLargest-coin-first is provably optimal for these sets

Interview tell: greedy problems almost always begin with "sort by something". If sorting by a clever key makes the right choice obvious at each step, you're likely in greedy territory.

#Essential Patterns

#Pattern 1 — Interval scheduling (sort by end)

Task: Given a set of time intervals, keep as many non-overlapping ones as possible.

Example: [[1,3], [2,4], [3,5]] → keep [1,3] and [3,5] (2 intervals; touching ends are fine)

(Full walkthrough: Problem 2 below.)

To fit the most non-overlapping intervals, always keep the one that finishes earliest — it frees the timeline soonest.

#Pattern 2 — Reach / jump (track the furthest reachable)

Task: Given how far you can jump from each position, decide whether you can reach the last index.

Example: nums = [2, 3, 1, 1, 4] → true; nums = [3, 2, 1, 0, 4] → false

(Full walkthrough: Problems 3 and 4 below.)

For "can I reach the end?" / "fewest jumps", sweep left to right tracking the furthest index reachable so far.

#Pattern 3 — Exchange argument (prove by swapping)

Task: Explain why a greedy rule is correct, not just that it passes the tests.

Example: (Assign Cookies) if a bigger cookie goes to a child who a smaller cookie would have satisfied, swapping the two never loses a satisfied child, so "give the smallest cookie that works" is safe.

To justify a greedy order, argue that swapping any two elements out of greedy order never improves (and often worsens) the result. This is the proof interviewers want to hear — even informally.

#Practice Problems

#Problem 1 — Assign Cookies (LeetCode #455)

Task: Each child i has greed g[i]; each cookie j has size s[j]. A child is content if s[j] >= g[i]. Maximise content children (one cookie each).

Example: g = [1,2,3], s = [1,1] → 1

Key insight: Sort both. Give the smallest adequate cookie to the least greedy child — wasting the least capacity.

Solution:

int FindContentChildren(int[] g, int[] s)
{
    Array.Sort(g);
    Array.Sort(s);
    int child = 0, cookie = 0;
    while (child < g.Length && cookie < s.Length)
    {
        if (s[cookie] >= g[child]) child++;  // this cookie satisfies this child
        cookie++;                            // either way, move past this cookie
    }
    return child;
}
def find_content_children(g, s):
    g.sort(); s.sort()
    child = cookie = 0
    while child < len(g) and cookie < len(s):
        if s[cookie] >= g[child]:
            child += 1
        cookie += 1
    return child

⏱ Time: O(n log n) | Space: O(1)

#Problem 2 — Non-overlapping Intervals (LeetCode #435)

Task: Given intervals, return the minimum number to remove so the rest don't overlap.

Example: [[1,2],[2,3],[3,4],[1,3]] → 1

Key insight: The classic interval-scheduling greedy. Sort by end; keep an interval only if it starts at or after the last kept interval's end. Everything you skip is a removal.

Solution:

int EraseOverlapIntervals(int[][] intervals)
{
    Array.Sort(intervals, (a, b) => a[1].CompareTo(b[1]));  // by end time
    int kept = 0, lastEnd = int.MinValue;
    foreach (var iv in intervals)
    {
        if (iv[0] >= lastEnd)   // no overlap → keep it
        {
            kept++;
            lastEnd = iv[1];
        }
    }
    return intervals.Length - kept;   // the rest had to be removed
}
def erase_overlap_intervals(intervals):
    intervals.sort(key=lambda iv: iv[1])   # by end time
    kept, last_end = 0, float('-inf')
    for s, e in intervals:
        if s >= last_end:
            kept += 1
            last_end = e
    return len(intervals) - kept

⏱ Time: O(n log n) | Space: O(1)

#Problem 3 — Jump Game (LeetCode #55)

Task: nums[i] is the max jump length from index i. Can you reach the last index?

Example: [2,3,1,1,4] → true

Key insight: Track the furthest index reachable. If you ever stand beyond it, you're stuck. No DP needed.

Solution:

bool CanJump(int[] nums)
{
    int furthest = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.Length; i++)
    {
        if (i > furthest) return false;            // can't even get here
        furthest = Math.Max(furthest, i + nums[i]); // extend our reach
    }
    return true;
}
def can_jump(nums):
    furthest = 0
    for i, n in enumerate(nums):
        if i > furthest:
            return False
        furthest = max(furthest, i + n)
    return True

⏱ Time: O(n) | Space: O(1)

#Problem 4 — Jump Game II (LeetCode #45)

Task: Same array, but return the minimum number of jumps to reach the last index (assume it's reachable).

Example: [2,3,1,1,4] → 2

Key insight: A greedy BFS "by level": within the current jump's reach, pre-compute the furthest you could reach with one more jump. When you hit the end of the current reach, you must spend a jump.

Solution:

int Jump(int[] nums)
{
    int jumps = 0, curEnd = 0, farthest = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.Length - 1; i++)   // no need to jump from the last index
    {
        farthest = Math.Max(farthest, i + nums[i]);
        if (i == curEnd)        // consumed the current jump's range
        {
            jumps++;
            curEnd = farthest;  // commit to the best next range
        }
    }
    return jumps;
}
def jump(nums):
    jumps = cur_end = farthest = 0
    for i in range(len(nums) - 1):
        farthest = max(farthest, i + nums[i])
        if i == cur_end:
            jumps += 1
            cur_end = farthest
    return jumps

⏱ Time: O(n) | Space: O(1)

#Common Mistakes

  • Assuming greedy works for min-coin-change. For coins [1, 3, 4] and amount 6, greedy takes 4 + 1 + 1 = 3 coins, but 3 + 3 = 2 coins is optimal. Arbitrary denominations need DP (09); greedy is only safe for canonical currency systems.
  • Sorting by the wrong key. Interval scheduling sorts by end, not start. Sorting by start maximises nothing.
  • No proof. "It felt right" isn't an argument. Have a one-line exchange argument ready, or you can't trust the answer.
  • Using greedy to count. "Number of ways" is never greedy — greedy commits to a single path and can't enumerate.
  • Forgetting the edge where greedy leaves you stranded. In reach problems, always check "am I now standing beyond the furthest reachable point?"

#LeetCode Problems to Try Now

  • #455 — Assign Cookies ⭐ (sort both, two pointers)
  • #55 — Jump Game ⭐ (furthest-reach sweep)
  • #435 — Non-overlapping Intervals ⭐⭐ (sort by end)
  • #45 — Jump Game II ⭐⭐ (greedy BFS by level)
  • #621 — Task Scheduler ⭐⭐ (greedy by most-frequent)
  • #134 — Gas Station ⭐⭐ (single-pass greedy with a proof)
  • #763 — Partition Labels (greedy by last-occurrence)

#Where to go next

Greedy and DP are the two answers to optimisation problems — always ask "is the greedy choice provably safe?" before committing. If it isn't, fall back to 13 — DP Patterns. Then let 14 — Pattern Recognition drill the signal → tool mapping until it's instant.

#16 — Tries (Prefix Trees)

A trie (pronounced "try", from retrieval) is a tree where each node represents one character and each root-to-node path spells a prefix. It turns "does any word start with app?" and "insert/search this word" into operations that cost O(length of the word) — independent of how many words you've stored.

Builds on: 08 — Trees & Recursion (a trie is a tree with a child per character) and 02 — Hash Maps & Sets (each node's children are a small map).

#Why a trie instead of a hash set?

A HashSet<string> answers "is this exact word present?" in O(1). But it's useless for prefixes — to find all words starting with "app" you'd scan every key. A trie is built for exactly that: shared prefixes are stored once, and prefix queries walk a single path.

  • Space win: app, apple, apply share the a-p-p spine — stored once.
  • Prefix win: "is there a word with prefix appl?" = walk 4 edges, done.

Reach for a trie when you see: autocomplete, "starts with", dictionary/word-search, spell-check, IP routing (longest-prefix match), or many strings sharing prefixes.

#The node + the three operations

A node holds a map of child characters and a flag marking whether a word ends here (so app can be a word even though it's also a prefix of apple).

#Pattern — build the node once, reuse everywhere

Task: Store a set of words so you can quickly check both whole words and prefixes.

Example: after inserting "app" and "apple": search("app") → true, search("appl") → false, startsWith("appl") → true

(Full walkthrough: Problem 1 below.)

insert, search, and startsWith are the same walk; they differ only in what they check at the end (word-end flag vs "did the path exist at all").

#Practice Problems

#Problem 1 — Implement Trie (LeetCode #208)

Task: Support insert(word), search(word) (exact word present?), and startsWith(prefix).

Example: insert("apple"), search("app") → false, startsWith("app") → true, insert("app"), search("app") → true

Key insight: One traversal method Walk(prefix) returns the node at the end of a path (or null). search additionally checks the end-of-word flag; startsWith just checks the node exists.

Solution:

public class Trie
{
    private class Node
    {
        public Dictionary<char, Node> Children = new();
        public bool IsWord;
    }

    private readonly Node _root = new();

    public void Insert(string word)
    {
        var cur = _root;
        foreach (char c in word)
        {
            if (!cur.Children.TryGetValue(c, out var next))
                cur.Children[c] = next = new Node();
            cur = next;
        }
        cur.IsWord = true;                 // mark the end of a complete word
    }

    private Node Walk(string s)            // node at end of path, or null
    {
        var cur = _root;
        foreach (char c in s)
        {
            if (!cur.Children.TryGetValue(c, out var next)) return null;
            cur = next;
        }
        return cur;
    }

    public bool Search(string word)    => Walk(word) is { IsWord: true };
    public bool StartsWith(string pre) => Walk(pre) is not null;
}
class Trie:
    def __init__(self):
        self.children = {}
        self.is_word = False

    def insert(self, word):
        cur = self
        for c in word:
            cur = cur.children.setdefault(c, Trie())
        cur.is_word = True

    def _walk(self, s):                    # node at end of path, or None
        cur = self
        for c in s:
            if c not in cur.children:
                return None
            cur = cur.children[c]
        return cur

    def search(self, word):
        node = self._walk(word)
        return node is not None and node.is_word

    def startsWith(self, prefix):
        return self._walk(prefix) is not None

⏱ Insert/Search/StartsWith: O(L) time, where L = word length | Space: O(total characters inserted)

#Problem 2 — Design Add and Search Words with . wildcard (LeetCode #211)

Task: addWord(word) and search(word) where word may contain . matching any single character.

Example: addWord("bad"), search("b.d") → true, search("b..") → true, search("b...") → false

Key insight: A plain walk fails on . because you don't know which child to follow — so branch: on ., recurse into every child. This is a DFS over the trie (08).

Solution:

public class WordDictionary
{
    private class Node { public Dictionary<char, Node> Children = new(); public bool IsWord; }
    private readonly Node _root = new();

    public void AddWord(string word)
    {
        var cur = _root;
        foreach (char c in word)
        {
            if (!cur.Children.TryGetValue(c, out var next))
                cur.Children[c] = next = new Node();
            cur = next;
        }
        cur.IsWord = true;
    }

    public bool Search(string word) => Dfs(word, 0, _root);

    private bool Dfs(string word, int i, Node node)
    {
        if (i == word.Length) return node.IsWord;
        char c = word[i];
        if (c == '.')                                   // wildcard: try every child
        {
            foreach (var child in node.Children.Values)
                if (Dfs(word, i + 1, child)) return true;
            return false;
        }
        return node.Children.TryGetValue(c, out var next) && Dfs(word, i + 1, next);
    }
}
class WordDictionary:
    def __init__(self):
        self.children = {}
        self.is_word = False

    def addWord(self, word):
        cur = self
        for c in word:
            cur = cur.children.setdefault(c, WordDictionary())
        cur.is_word = True

    def search(self, word):
        def dfs(i, node):
            if i == len(word):
                return node.is_word
            c = word[i]
            if c == '.':
                return any(dfs(i + 1, ch) for ch in node.children.values())
            return c in node.children and dfs(i + 1, node.children[c])
        return dfs(0, self)

⏱ Time: O(L) typical, O(26^d · L) worst with d wildcards | Space: O(total characters)

#Common Mistakes

  • Forgetting the end-of-word flag. Without it you can't tell "app is a stored word" from "app is only a prefix of apple." search and insert both depend on it.
  • Using a fixed 26-slot array when the alphabet isn't lowercase a–z. A Dictionary/map of children is safer for mixed/Unicode input; the array is a micro-optimisation for known small alphabets.
  • Confusing search and startsWith. They walk identically; only the final check differs (word-end flag vs node-exists).
  • Recursing on every child when there's no wildcard. Branch only on .; a concrete character follows exactly one edge.

#LeetCode Problems to Try Now

  • #208 — Implement Trie ⭐ (the canonical build)
  • #211 — Add and Search Word with . ⭐⭐ (DFS branching on wildcard)
  • #212 — Word Search II ⭐⭐ (trie + grid backtracking — pairs with 12 — Backtracking)
  • #648 — Replace Words (longest-prefix replacement)
  • #1268 — Search Suggestions System (autocomplete)

#Where to go next

Tries shine the moment "prefix" or "autocomplete" appears — that's the tell to add to your 14 — Pattern Recognition cheat sheet. The hardest trie problems (e.g. Word Search II) combine the trie with backtracking: the trie prunes the search the instant a path can't spell any word.

#17 — Advanced Two Pointers

The Two-Pointer technique is often the "missing link" between a brute-force O(n2) solution and an optimal O(n) implementation. While the basic concept—tracking two indices—is simple, its advanced applications involve managing complex state, coordinating three or more pointers, and handling non-linear data traversal.

This guide focuses on the "Senior" applications of the technique: partitioning unsorted data, expanding from centers, and maintaining running maximums to solve multi-faceted problems like water trapping and triplet sum optimization.

#Core Concepts

#When to use it

  • The simple "left/right" or "fast/slow" isn't enough to capture the state.
  • You need to track multiple boundaries (e.g., 3Sum, Trapping Water).
  • The array is unsorted but can be partitioned (e.g., Dutch National Flag).

#The Flavors

1. The "Middle-Out" approach — starting at a pivot and expanding (common in substring problems). 2. Three-Pointer partitioning — used to sort or group elements into three categories. 3. The "Stateful" pointer — one pointer moves normally while the other only moves when a complex condition is met.

#Patterns with Code

#Pattern 1: Three-way partitioning (Dutch National Flag)

// Using a while loop for manual control over pointer increments
void ThreeWayPartition(int[] nums, int midVal)
{
    int low = 0, mid = 0, high = nums.Length - 1;
    while (mid <= high)
    {
        if (nums[mid] < midVal)
            (nums[low++], nums[mid++]) = (nums[mid], nums[low]);
        else if (nums[mid] == midVal)
            mid++;
        else
            (nums[mid], nums[high--]) = (nums[high], nums[mid]);
    }
}

Explanation: This uses three pointers to divide an array into three sections: elements less than, equal to, and greater than a pivot (midVal).

  • low: Boundary for the "less than" section.
  • mid: The current element being scanned.
  • high: Boundary for the "greater than" section.

Reasoning: Standard two-pointer logic only handles two states (e.g., "yes" or "no"). Adding a third pointer allows you to sort or group elements with three distinct states in a single pass (O(n) time) without using extra space (O(1) space).

#Pattern 2: Fixed-Ahead Pointer (Fast & Slow)

// Using a for loop when the 'fast' pointer moves predictably
int RemoveElement(int[] nums, int val) {
    int slow = 0;
    for (int fast = 0; fast < nums.Length; fast++) {
        if (nums[fast] != val) {
            nums[slow++] = nums[fast];
        }
    }
    return slow;
}

Explanation: The fast pointer (controlled by the for loop) scans every element, while the slow pointer only moves when a condition is met (in this case, when the element isn't the one we want to remove).

Reasoning: For loops are cleaner when one pointer moves linearly through the entire collection. It reduces the risk of infinite loops and clearly separates the "scanner" (fast) from the "builder" (slow).

#Pattern 3: The "Expand from Center" (Palindrome Substrings)

int ExpandAroundCenter(string s, int left, int right)
{
    while (left >= 0 && right < s.Length && s[left] == s[right])
    {
        left--;
        right++;
    }
    return right - left - 1; // Returns length of the palindrome
}

Explanation: Instead of moving inward from the ends, you start at a potential center (a single character or the gap between two) and push the pointers outward as long as the characters match.

Reasoning: This is the most efficient way to find palindromic substrings. By treating every index as a potential "center," you avoid the redundant checks of a sliding window and handle both odd-length (center is one char) and even-length (center is between two chars) palindromes elegantly.

#Building Blocks: Warm-up Exercises

These exercises bridge the gap between simple concepts and LeetCode-level challenges. Complete the code skeletons provided to master pointer coordination.

#Exercise 1 — The Intersection of Two Sorted Arrays

Task: Given two sorted arrays, find the elements that appear in both. Goal: Learn how to move pointers independently based on a comparison.

The "Half-Way" Guide:

public List<int> FindIntersection(int[] arr1, int[] arr2) {
    int i = 0, j = 0;
    var result = new List<int>();

    while (i < arr1.Length && j < arr2.Length) {
        if (arr1[i] == arr2[j]) {
            result.Add(arr1[i]);
            i++; j++;
        }
        else if (arr1[i] < arr2[j]) {
            // Your Mission: One array's value is too small to ever match the other. 
            // Which pointer should move forward?
        }
        else {
            // Your Mission: The other array's value is too small. Move it!
        }
    }
    return result;
}

#Exercise 2 — Targeted Partitioning (The "Gatekeeper")

Task: Given an array, move all occurrences of a specific target to the front without using extra space. Goal: Master the "Write" vs "Read" pointer dynamic.

The "Half-Way" Guide:

void MoveTargetToFront(int[] nums, int target) {
    int write = 0;
    // We use a for loop as a 'scanner' (read pointer)
    for (int read = 0; read < nums.Length; read++) {
        if (nums[read] == target) {
            // Your Mission: 
            // 1. Swap nums[read] with the element at the 'write' position
            // 2. Increment 'write' to protect the target you just moved
            
            (nums[write], nums[read]) = (nums[read], nums[write]);
            // ... what next?
        }
    }
}

#Practice Problems

#Problem 1 — Container With Most Water (LeetCode #11)

Task: Find two lines that together with the x-axis forms a container that holds the most water.

Example: [1, 8, 6, 2, 5, 4, 8, 3, 7] → 49

Hints & Tips:

  • The Constraint: The area is always limited by the shorter of the two lines.
  • Pointer Movement: To find a potentially larger area, you must move the pointer at the shorter line. Moving the taller line inward can only decrease the width without ever increasing the height.
  • Formula: Area = min(height[left], height[right]) * (right - left).

#Problem 2 — 3Sum (LeetCode #15)

Task: Find all unique triplets that sum to zero.

Example: [-1, 0, 1, 2, -1, -4] → [[-1, -1, 2], [-1, 0, 1]]

Hints & Tips:

  • Pre-requisite: Sorting is mandatory here to handle duplicates efficiently and use the two-pointer logic.
  • Reduction: Fix one number i with a loop, then treat the rest of the array as a "Two Sum II" problem (finding two numbers that sum to -nums[i]).
  • Duplicate Handling: After finding a valid triplet, move both pointers past any identical values to avoid adding the same triplet twice.

#Problem 3 — Trapping Rain Water (LeetCode #42)

Task: Compute how much water can be trapped after raining.

Example: [0,1,0,2,1,0,1,3,2,1,2,1] → 6

Hints & Tips:

  • The Logic: At any index i, the water trapped is determined by the min(tallest_bar_to_left, tallest_bar_to_right) - height[i].
  • Efficiency: Instead of pre-calculating arrays for left/right maxes, use two pointers from the ends. Keep track of leftMax and rightMax as you move.
  • Decision: If height[left] < height[right], the water level at left is bottled by leftMax. Process the left side and move inward.

#Common Mistakes

  • Not skipping duplicates in problems like 3Sum — leads to duplicate results and extra work.
  • Forgetting to sort — advanced two-pointer patterns almost always require a pre-sorted array.
  • Incorrect pointer updates — especially in problems like Trapping Rain Water, ensure you update the max before calculating volume.

#LeetCode Problems to Try Now

  • #11 — Container With Most Water ⭐
  • #15 — 3Sum ⭐⭐
  • #42 — Trapping Rain Water ⭐⭐⭐
  • #75 — Sort Colors (Dutch National Flag)
  • #18 — 4Sum

#18 — Space Complexity: The Memory Blueprint

Space complexity is the measure of how much working memory (RAM) an algorithm needs to complete its task. For a student, the best way to think about this is: "If I give my program a massive amount of data, will my computer run out of memory and crash?"

#1. The Two Components of Space

To truly understand space, you must distinguish between what you are given and what you create.

#A. Input Space (The Given)

This is the memory taken up by the data passed into your function.

  • Analogy: If you are a chef, this is the pile of raw ingredients sitting on your counter. You didn't buy them; they were provided to you.

#B. Auxiliary Space (The Extra)

This is the additional memory your algorithm uses to solve the problem.

  • Analogy: These are the extra bowls, blenders, and storage containers you take out of the cupboard to prepare the meal.
  • Critical Interview Tip: When an interviewer asks for "Space Complexity," they almost always mean Auxiliary Space.

#2. The Big-O Hierarchy of Space

Think of Space Complexity as the "Floor Space" you need to solve a puzzle. As the puzzle gets bigger, how much more floor space do you need to clear out?

#🟦 O(1) — Constant Space: "The Pocket"

No matter how many items you are processing, you only need a fixed amount of memory. You aren't creating new collections; you're just using a few "sticky notes" to keep track of things.

  • The Mental Model: You are sitting at a small desk. Whether the library delivers 5 books or 5,000, you only have room for one book at a time on your desk.
  • When it happens:
    • Using a few variables (int i, int sum, bool found).
    • Swapping elements in an array using a single temp variable.
    • Using Two Pointers (left and right) to scan a list.
  • C# Example:
bool ContainsZero(int[] nums) {
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.Length; i++) { // 'i' is O(1) space
        if (nums[i] == 0) return true;
    }
    return false;
}

#🟩 O(log n) — Logarithmic Space: "The Fold"

This is common in "Divide and Conquer" algorithms. Memory usage increases by one unit every time the input doubles.

  • The Mental Model: Imagine a phone book. To find a name, you open it in the middle, then in the middle of the remaining half, and so on. Even if the phone book has 1 million names, you only need to remember about 20 "branching points" to reach your target.
  • When it happens:
    • Recursive Binary Search (storing stack frames for each "half").
    • Traversing a balanced binary tree.
  • Visual:
Input Size:  8 -> [][][] (3 units)
Input Size: 16 -> [][][][] (4 units)
Input Size: 32 -> [][][][][] (5 units)

#🟨 O(n) — Linear Space: "The Mirror"

The extra memory you use is a direct reflection of the input size. If you get twice as much data, you use twice as much memory.

  • The Mental Model: You are building a backup of a photo album. For every photo in the original album, you need exactly one slot in the new album.
  • When it happens:
    • Creating a copy of an array.
    • Using a List<T> or HashSet<T> to store elements from the input.
    • Recursion where the depth is equal to N (e.g., recursive factorial).
  • C# Example:
int[] SquareAll(int[] nums) {
    int[] results = new int[nums.Length]; // New array of size N = O(n) space
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.Length; i++) results[i] = nums[i] * nums[i];
    return results;
}

#🟥 O(n2) — Quadratic Space: "The Grid"

Memory usage grows with the square of the input. This is usually very expensive and should be avoided if possible.

  • The Mental Model: You are creating a seating chart for a wedding. If you have N guests, and you want to record whether Guest A likes Guest B for every possible pair, you need an N × N grid.
  • When it happens:
    • Creating a 2D matrix of size N × N.
    • Building an adjacency matrix for a graph with N nodes.
  • Visual:
Input N=3:  [ ][ ][ ]
            [ ][ ][ ]
            [ ][ ][ ]  (9 total slots)

#3. The "Hidden" Culprit: The Call Stack

Many students forget that functions themselves take up space. When you call a function, the computer must remember:

  1. Where it was in the previous function (Return Address).
  2. What the local variables were at that moment.
  3. What parameters were passed in.

This "bundle" of info is called a Stack Frame.

#Visualizing Recursive Space

Consider a recursive function calculating the sum of numbers from N down to 1:

int Sum(int n) {
    if (n <= 0) return 0;
    return n + Sum(n - 1); // <--- Each call here waits!
}

If N = 100, the computer has to hold 100 "active" functions in memory at the same time because the first one can't finish until the 100th one returns.

  • Complexity: O(n) Auxiliary Space.
  • Optimization: Switching this to a for loop uses O(1) space because you only ever have one function active.

#4. How to Calculate Space Complexity (Step-by-Step)

Calculating space complexity is like performing a "Memory Audit" on your code. Follow these four steps to find the Big-O:

#Step 1: Identify all created variables

Look at every variable, array, list, or object created inside your function.

#Step 2: Determine dependency on 'N'

For each item identified in Step 1, ask: "If the input N doubles, does this variable's memory requirement also double, stay the same, or grow some other way?"

  • Static variables (ints, bools, single objects) are O(1).
  • Dynamic collections (arrays, lists, maps) of size N are O(n).

#Step 3: Audit the Call Stack

If your code is recursive, find the maximum depth of the recursion. Each level of recursion adds a new frame to the stack.

  • A recursion that goes N levels deep is O(n) space.

#Step 4: Keep the Dominant Term

Just like in time complexity, we only care about the fastest-growing part.

  • O(n) + O(1) → O(n)
  • O(n2) + O(n) → O(n2)

#Walkthrough Example: The "Memory Auditor" in Action

Code to Analyze:

public List<string> FilterAndUpper(string[] words) {
    var result = new List<string>(); // 1. Created a list
    int count = 0;                  // 2. Created an int

    foreach (var word in words) {
        if (word.Length > 5) {
            result.Add(word.ToUpper()); // 3. Creating new strings
            count++;
        }
    }
    return result;
}

The Audit:

  1. result list: In the worst case (all words > 5 chars), this list will store N strings. -> O(n)
  2. count variable: Just a single integer. It doesn't grow if the input grows. -> O(1)
  3. word.ToUpper(): This creates a new string for every word added to the list. Since these are stored in the list, they are part of the O(n) growth.
  4. Final Calculation: O(n) + O(1) = O(n) Auxiliary Space.
  1. Physical Limits: Unlike time (which just makes you wait), memory is a hard limit. If you exceed the RAM, your program crashes with an OutOfMemoryException.
  2. Embedded Systems: If you are writing code for a microwave, a smartwatch, or a car's braking system, you might only have a few kilobytes of memory to work with.
  3. Cloud Costs: In modern cloud computing (AWS/Azure), you are often billed based on the amount of memory your functions consume.

#5. Summary Table for Quick Reference

ComplexityAnalogyCommon C# Usage
O(1)A single sticky note.int, bool, double, pointers.
O(log n)A small stack of index cards.Recursive Binary Search, Balanced Tree traversal.
O(n)A notebook with one page per item.Array, List<T>, Dictionary<K,V>, Recursion.
O(n2)A massive floor grid.2D Arrays (Matrices), Adjacency Matrices for Graphs.

#Practice Questions (Check Your Understanding)

Q1: You use two pointers to swap elements in an array to reverse it. What is the Auxiliary Space?

  • Answer: O(1). You only used two integer variables (left and right), regardless of how big the array was.

Q2: You have a list of N names. You want to find if there are any duplicates, so you add each name to a HashSet<string>. What is the space complexity?

  • Answer: O(n). In the worst case (no duplicates), your HashSet will eventually store all N names.

Q3: Why is recursion generally "riskier" for space than iteration?

  • Answer: Because recursion uses the Call Stack. Each recursive call adds a new frame to memory, whereas a loop reuse the same variables over and over in a single frame.

#🎯 Interview Prep — Mid/Senior Engineering Roles

Generic preparation for software engineering interviews at top tech companies. Level: Mid to Senior Software Engineer (IC3–IC5)

#📚 Contents

💡 New here? Read System Design Building Blocks first. It's the reusable toolkit (load balancing, caching, replication, partitioning, consistency, messaging, resilience) that the worked questions below assemble. Learn the blocks once, then the question-driven chapters show you how to compose them.

#TopicFocus
1System DesignScalable architectures, data flow, trade-offs
2Coding & AlgorithmsLive coding, problem decomposition, optimal solutions
3Behavioural & CultureOwnership, collaboration, incident response, leadership
4API Design & Distributed SystemsRESTful APIs, event-driven architecture, consistency
5Database & Data ModellingSchema design, transactions, scaling patterns
6System Design Building BlocksFoundational toolkit — the reusable components + DDIA reading map

📖 Chapter 6 doubles as a reading map for Designing Data-Intensive Applications (Kleppmann), pointing each building block to the DDIA chapter that explains the theory beneath it.

#🏢 What Top Tech Companies Look For

Technical Bar:

  • Strong fundamentals (data structures, algorithms, complexity)
  • System design at scale (millions of users, high throughput)
  • API-first thinking — clean contracts, versioning, backward compatibility
  • Understanding of distributed systems trade-offs (CAP, eventual consistency)

Engineering Culture:

  • Ownership — you build it, you run it
  • Collaboration — pairing, code review, RFCs
  • Impact orientation — delivering measurable outcomes
  • Incident response — structured debugging, clear communication under pressure
  • Simplicity — prefer well-understood solutions over clever ones

Interview Format (typical):

  1. Phone screen — 30–45 min coding or system design
  2. Take-home or live coding — 60–90 min practical problem
  3. System design — 45–60 min whiteboard/virtual
  4. Behavioural — 45 min values & collaboration
  5. Bar raiser / team fit — cross-team assessment

#🗺️ How to Use This Guide

  1. Start with System Design if you're senior — it's weighted heavily
  2. Start with Coding if you're mid-level — it's the gatekeeper
  3. Don't skip Behavioural — every company cares about culture fit
  4. Work through each doc's questions, attempt answers before reading the solutions
  5. Practice explaining your reasoning out loud — interviewers assess communication as much as correctness

#🧭 Maintaining these docs (contributor note)

This set is the canonical home for general interview-prep theory. Domain tracks (e.g. interview-prep-monzo) are thin overlays: they link back to the canonical section here and add only domain-specific deltas.

Rules to prevent duplication drift:

  • A concept lives in one place. If it's general (idempotency, Outbox, circuit breaker, versioning, index-selection rules, locking strategies, the latency table), it belongs here and carries a 📌 Canonical source banner.
  • Domain tracks must link to the canonical section, not copy it, and add only what's genuinely domain-specific (marked as a "delta").
  • If you fix a canonical section, you fix it once — no parallel copies to chase.
  • Deliberately different domain values (e.g. fintech scaling thresholds) must be labelled as intentional with a one-line reason, so they don't read as contradictions.

#01 — System Design

System design is the highest-weighted interview for senior roles. You'll be asked to design systems that handle millions of users, high throughput, and complex data flows.

#Framework for Answering

Use this structure for every system design question:

1. Clarify Requirements (2–3 min)
   → Functional: what does the system DO?
   → Non-functional: scale, latency, consistency, availability

2. Back-of-envelope Estimation (2 min)
   → Users, requests/sec, storage, bandwidth

3. High-Level Design (10 min)
   → Core components, data flow diagram

4. Deep Dive (15–20 min)
   → Pick 2–3 components to detail
   → Data model, APIs, trade-offs

5. Trade-offs & Extensions (5 min)
   → What breaks at 10x scale?
   → What would you monitor/alert on?

#Question 1 — Design a URL Shortener

"Design a service like bit.ly that shortens URLs and redirects users."

#Clarifying Questions to Ask

  • What's the expected volume of URLs created per day?
  • What's the read:write ratio?
  • Do short URLs expire?
  • Do we need analytics (click counts, referrers)?

#Requirements

  • Functional: Create short URL, redirect to original, optional analytics
  • Non-functional: Low latency redirects (<50ms), high availability, 100:1 read:write ratio

#Back-of-Envelope

- 100M URLs created/month
- 10B redirects/month (100:1 ratio)
- ~3,800 writes/sec, ~380,000 reads/sec at peak
- Each URL record: ~500 bytes
- Storage: 100M × 500B × 12 months = 600GB/year

#High-Level Architecture

flowchart LR C([Client]) --> G["API Gateway<br/>(Rate limit)"] --> U[URL Service] U --> Cache[("Cache<br/>Redis")] U --> DB[("Database<br/>Postgres")] U --> AN["Analytics<br/>Kafka → ClickHouse"]

#Key Design Decisions

Short URL Generation:

// Option 1: Base62 encoding of auto-increment ID
string Encode(long id)
{
    const string chars = "0123456789abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyzABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZ";
    var sb = new StringBuilder();
    while (id > 0)
    {
        sb.Insert(0, chars[(int)(id % 62)]);
        id /= 62;
    }
    return sb.ToString();
}
// 7 chars = 62^7 = 3.5 trillion unique URLs

// Option 2: Hash-based (MD5/SHA → take first 7 chars)
// Pro: No coordination needed. Con: Collisions possible.

Read Path (optimised for speed):

1. Client requests GET /abc1234
2. Check Redis cache → hit? Return 301 redirect
3. Cache miss → query Postgres → cache result → return 301
4. Async: publish click event to Kafka for analytics

#Trade-offs

DecisionChoiceWhy
301 vs 302 redirect301 (permanent)Browsers cache it; reduces load. Use 302 if analytics matter more
ID generationDistributed ID generator (Snowflake)Avoids single-point-of-failure of auto-increment
Cache evictionLRU with TTLMost URLs follow power-law distribution (few get most traffic)
DatabasePostgresSimple key-value lookup; could also use DynamoDB for scale

📖 DDIA: the read-path cache + replica reads raise the stale-read questions from Ch. 5 (Replication); distributed ID generation (Snowflake) and avoiding auto-increment coordination connect to Ch. 6 (Partitioning) and Ch. 9 (Consistency and Consensus). See the System Design Building Blocks chapter for the reusable toolkit behind these choices.

#Question 2 — Design a Notification System

"Design a system that sends push notifications, emails, and SMS to users."

#Requirements

  • Functional: Multi-channel delivery (push, email, SMS), user preferences, templating, scheduling
  • Non-functional: At-least-once delivery, <5 second push latency, handle 100K notifications/sec at peak

#High-Level Architecture

#Event Flow

1. Service publishes event (e.g., "order.shipped") → Kafka
2. Notification orchestrator consumes event
3. Looks up user preferences (which channels? quiet hours?)
4. Renders template with event data
5. Routes to appropriate delivery worker (push/email/SMS)
6. Worker delivers via third-party provider
7. Stores delivery status for audit/retry

#Key Design Decisions

Why Kafka?

  • Decouples producers from notification logic
  • Handles burst traffic via buffering
  • Replay capability if notification service goes down
  • Multiple consumers from same event stream

Handling Failures:

Worker attempts delivery → fails
  → Retry with exponential backoff (1s, 2s, 4s, 8s, 16s)
  → After 5 retries → dead letter queue (DLQ)
  → Alert on DLQ depth > threshold
  → Manual review / batch retry

Priority Queues:

High priority:   Security alerts, OTP codes     → dedicated workers, no batching
Medium priority: Order updates, shipping         → standard workers
Low priority:    Marketing, weekly digests       → batch processing, respect quiet hours

#Question 3 — Design a Rate Limiter

"Design a distributed rate limiter that protects your API from abuse."

#Requirements

  • Functional: Limit requests per user/IP per time window, configurable per endpoint
  • Non-functional: <1ms overhead per request, distributed (works across multiple API servers)

#Algorithms Compared

AlgorithmProsCons
Fixed windowSimple, low memoryBurst at window boundary (2x allowed)
Sliding window logPreciseHigh memory (stores every timestamp)
Sliding window counterGood balanceSlight approximation
Token bucketSmooth, allows burstsSlightly more complex
Leaky bucketConstant output rateDoesn't allow legitimate bursts

#Token Bucket Implementation (Redis)

public class TokenBucketRateLimiter
{
    private readonly IDatabase _redis;
    private readonly int _maxTokens;
    private readonly int _refillRate; // tokens per second

    public async Task<bool> IsAllowed(string key)
    {
        var now = DateTimeOffset.UtcNow.ToUnixTimeMilliseconds();
        var script = @"
            local key = KEYS[1]
            local max_tokens = tonumber(ARGV[1])
            local refill_rate = tonumber(ARGV[2])
            local now = tonumber(ARGV[3])

            local data = redis.call('HMGET', key, 'tokens', 'last_refill')
            local tokens = tonumber(data[1]) or max_tokens
            local last_refill = tonumber(data[2]) or now

            -- Refill tokens based on elapsed time
            local elapsed = (now - last_refill) / 1000.0
            tokens = math.min(max_tokens, tokens + elapsed * refill_rate)

            if tokens >= 1 then
                tokens = tokens - 1
                redis.call('HMSET', key, 'tokens', tokens, 'last_refill', now)
                redis.call('EXPIRE', key, max_tokens / refill_rate * 2)
                return 1
            else
                redis.call('HMSET', key, 'tokens', tokens, 'last_refill', now)
                return 0
            end
        ";
        var result = await _redis.ScriptEvaluateAsync(script,
            new RedisKey[] { $"ratelimit:{key}" },
            new RedisValue[] { _maxTokens, _refillRate, now });
        return (int)result == 1;
    }
}

#Trade-offs

DecisionChoiceWhy
Where to limitAPI gateway layerSingle enforcement point; services don't need to implement
StorageRedisSub-millisecond reads; atomic operations via Lua scripts
GranularityPer-user + per-endpointPrevents one endpoint from consuming another's budget
Response on limit429 + Retry-After headerClient knows when to retry

#Question 4 — Design a Chat System

"Design a real-time messaging system supporting 1:1 and group chats."

#Requirements

  • Functional: Send/receive messages in real-time, message history, read receipts, online status
  • Non-functional: <200ms message delivery, message ordering, offline message delivery

#High-Level Architecture

#Message Flow

1. Alice sends message via WebSocket connection
2. WebSocket gateway routes to Chat Service
3. Chat Service:
   a. Persists message to Cassandra
   b. Checks if Bob is online (Presence Service / Redis)
   c. If online → route to Bob's WebSocket gateway → deliver
   d. If offline → queue for push notification
4. Bob's client acknowledges receipt → update delivery status

#Data Model (Cassandra — optimised for chat reads)

-- Messages partitioned by conversation, ordered by time
CREATE TABLE messages (
    conversation_id UUID,
    message_id      TIMEUUID,
    sender_id       UUID,
    content         TEXT,
    message_type    TEXT,       -- 'text', 'image', 'file'
    created_at      TIMESTAMP,
    PRIMARY KEY (conversation_id, message_id)
) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (message_id DESC);

-- User's conversation list
CREATE TABLE user_conversations (
    user_id             UUID,
    last_message_at     TIMESTAMP,
    conversation_id     UUID,
    last_message_preview TEXT,
    unread_count        INT,
    PRIMARY KEY (user_id, last_message_at)
) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (last_message_at DESC);

#Key Design Decisions

DecisionChoiceWhy
ProtocolWebSocket (with HTTP fallback)Bidirectional, low latency, persistent connection
Message storeCassandraWrite-heavy, time-series data, horizontal scaling
PresenceRedis with TTLFast pub/sub, automatic expiry for stale sessions
Message orderingServer-assigned timestamps (TIMEUUID)Client clocks can't be trusted
Group messagesFan-out on write (small groups) / fan-out on read (large channels)Trade-off between write amplification and read latency

#Question 5 — Design a Task Queue / Job Scheduler

"Design a system that executes background jobs reliably — retries on failure, supports scheduling, and scales horizontally."

#Requirements

  • Functional: Submit jobs, execute asynchronously, retry on failure, schedule for future, priority levels
  • Non-functional: At-least-once execution, horizontal scaling, job deduplication

#Architecture

#Job Lifecycle

PENDING → RUNNING → COMPLETED
                  → FAILED → RETRY (back to PENDING with delay)
                           → DEAD (max retries exceeded)

#Implementation

public class Job
{
    public string Id { get; set; }
    public string Type { get; set; }
    public string Payload { get; set; }       // JSON
    public int Priority { get; set; }
    public int Attempts { get; set; }
    public int MaxRetries { get; set; } = 3;
    public DateTime? ScheduledAt { get; set; }
    public DateTime? StartedAt { get; set; }
    public string Status { get; set; }        // pending, running, completed, failed, dead
}

public class Worker
{
    public async Task ProcessLoop(CancellationToken ct)
    {
        while (!ct.IsCancellationRequested)
        {
            var job = await _queue.Dequeue(timeout: TimeSpan.FromSeconds(5));
            if (job == null) continue;

            try
            {
                job.Status = "running";
                job.StartedAt = DateTime.UtcNow;
                job.Attempts++;
                await _store.Update(job);

                await ExecuteJob(job);

                job.Status = "completed";
                await _store.Update(job);
            }
            catch (Exception ex)
            {
                if (job.Attempts >= job.MaxRetries)
                    job.Status = "dead";
                else
                {
                    job.Status = "pending";
                    job.ScheduledAt = DateTime.UtcNow.Add(GetBackoff(job.Attempts));
                    await _queue.Enqueue(job, job.ScheduledAt);
                }
                await _store.Update(job);
            }
        }
    }

    private TimeSpan GetBackoff(int attempt) =>
        TimeSpan.FromSeconds(Math.Pow(2, attempt)); // 2s, 4s, 8s, 16s
}

#Key Design Decisions

DecisionChoiceWhy
Queue backendRedis (ZSET for delayed jobs) or SQSRedis for low latency; SQS for managed durability
Visibility timeoutLock job for 5 min while processingPrevents duplicate execution if worker crashes
DeduplicationUnique job ID; reject if already existsPrevents duplicate work from retried submissions
ScalingWorkers auto-scale based on queue depthCost-efficient; handles traffic spikes

#General Tips for System Design Interviews

  1. Always start with requirements — don't jump to architecture
  2. State your assumptions — "I'm assuming 10M users, is that reasonable?"
  3. Draw before you talk — diagrams communicate faster than words
  4. Name specific technologies — "I'd use Kafka here because..." not "some message queue"
  5. Discuss trade-offs unprompted — this is what separates senior from mid
  6. Think about failure modes — "What happens when this service goes down?"
  7. Consider the human side — "How would on-call engineers debug this?"
  8. Know your numbers:
    • Redis read: <1ms
    • Postgres read: 5–20ms
    • Kafka publish: 5–10ms
    • HTTP call (same region): 1–5ms
    • Cross-region: 50–150ms
    • SSD random read: 0.1ms
    • HDD random read: 10ms
